=============================================================================\n# CAM PROFILE FUNCTION \u2014 V5 Final\n# =============================================================================\ndef cam_radius(theta_deg):\n    t = theta_deg % 180.0\n    if t < 50.0:\n        x = t / 50.0\n        return R_BASE + insert_pocket(center_ang_deg, body):\\n    half  = INSERT_CHORD / 2.0\\n    z0    = (CAM_H - INSERT_H) / 2.0\\n    pin_r = INSERT_R_IN + STITCH_Z / 2.0\\n\\n    slot = Part.makeBox(SLOT_W, STITCH_Z, body = body.cut(ph)\\n\\n    ss_len = CAM_WALL - 1.5\\n    for chord_off in [-half * 0.5, half * 0.5]:\\n        ss = Part.makeCylinder(1.6, ss_len)\\n        ss.rotate(FreeCAD.Vector(0,0,0), height Z\nTOP_BEARING_POCKET_D = BEARING_OD + PRESS_FIT   # 27.95mm\nTOP_BEARING_POCKET_H = BEARING_W + 0.5          # 8.5mm deep\nTOP_BOLT_PCD    = 48.0\nTOP_BOLT_D      = 5.5     # M5 clearance\nTOP_BOLT_CS_D   = 10.0    # M5 countersink\n\n# =============================================================================\n# BOTTOM BLOCK PARAMETERS (larger, more load)\n# =============================================================================\nBOT_W           = 70.0\nBOT_D           = 70.0\nBOT_H           = THRUST_H + BEARING_W * 2 + 5.0   # 30mm total\n\nBOT_RADIAL_POCKET_D = BEARING_OD + PRESS_FIT        # 27.95mm (radial bearings)\nBOT_RADIAL_POCKET_H = BEARING_W * 2                 # 16mm (2 stacked)\nBOT_THRUST_POCKET_D = THRUST_OD + POCKET_SLIP       # 26.3mm (thrust, not press)\nBOT_THRUST_POCKET_H = THRUST_H                      # 9mm\nBOT_BOLT_PCD        = 56.0\nBOT_BOLT_D          = 5.5\nBOT_BOLT_CS_D       = 10.0\n\n# Both blocks \u2014 common features\nCHAMFER_SIZE    = 1.0    # outer edge chamfer for print quality\n\n# =============================================================================\n# BUILD TOP BLOCK\n# =============================================================================\nprint(\"=\" * 60)\nprint(\"CSM V3 \u2014 BEARING HOUSING BLOCKS V1\")\nprint(\"=\" * 60)\nprint(f\"\\nBuilding TOP block...\")\n\ntop = Part.makeBox(TOP_W, TOP_D, TOP_H)\ntop.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(-TOP_W / 2.0, -TOP_D / 2.0, 0))\n\n# Bearing pocket (top face, open top \u2014 bearing press-fits from above)\npocket = Part.makeCylinder(TOP_BEARING_POCKET_D / 2.0, TOP_BEARING_POCKET_H)\npocket.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(0, 0, TOP_H - TOP_BEARING_POCKET_H))\ntop = top.cut(pocket)\n\n# Shaft through-hole (full height, clearance for shaft)\nthrough = Part.makeCylinder(SHAFT_CLEAR / 2.0, TOP_H + 2)\nthrough.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(0, 0, -1))\ntop = top.cut(through)\n\n# 4\u00d7 M5 bolt holes with countersink on bottom (mount face)\nfor i in range(4):\n    ang = math.radians(i * 90.0 + 45.0)\n    bx = (TOP_BOLT_PCD / 2.0) * math.cos(ang)\n    by = (TOP_BOLT_PCD / 2.0) * math.sin(ang)\n    bh = Part.makeCylinder(TOP_BOLT_D / 2.0, TOP_H + 2)\n    bh.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(bx, by, -1))\n    top = top.cut(bh)\n    cs = Part.makeCylinder(TOP_BOLT_CS_D / 2.0, 3.5)\n    cs.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(bx, by, 0))\n    top = top.cut(cs)\n\n# Position TOP block separately for visualization\ntop.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(-50, 0, 0))\n\nobj_top = doc.addObject(\"Part::Feature\", \"BearingHousing_TOP\")\nobj_top.Shape = top\nobj_top.ViewObject.ShapeColor = (0.25, 0.55, 0.85)\nprint(f\"  TOP: {TOP_W}x{TOP_D}x{TOP_H}mm\")\nprint(f\"  Bearing pocket: \u00d8{TOP_BEARING_POCKET_D}mm \u00d7 {TOP_BEARING_POCKET_H}mm\")\nprint(f\"  Shaft bore: \u00d8{SHAFT_CLEAR}mm\")\nprint(f\"  Volume: {top.Volume:.0f}mm\u00b3\")\n\n# =============================================================================\n# BUILD BOTTOM BLOCK\n# =============================================================================\nprint(f\"\\nBuilding BOTTOM block...\")\n\nbot = Part.makeBox(BOT_W, BOT_D, BOT_H)\nbot.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(-BOT_W / 2.0, -BOT_D / 2.0, 0))\n\n# Thrust bearing pocket (BOTTOM, deepest) \u2014 slip fit, not press\n#\n# STACK FROM BOTTOM UP:\n#   Z=0 to Z=3: solid floor (3mm)\n#   Z=3 to Z=12: thrust bearing pocket (9mm)\n#   Z=12 to Z=28: radial bearing pocket (16mm = 2 stacked bearings)\n#   Z=28 to Z=30: top face\n\nthrust_pocket = Part.makeCylinder(BOT_THRUST_POCKET_D / 2.0, BOT_THRUST_POCKET_H)\nthrust_pocket.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(0, 0, 3.0))\nbot = bot.cut(thrust_pocket)\n\n# Radial bearing pocket (stacked 2\u00d7 bearings) \u2014 press fit\nradial_pocket = Part.makeCylinder(BOT_RADIAL_POCKET_D / 2.0, BOT_RADIAL_POCKET_H)\nradial_pocket.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(0, 0, 3.0 + BOT_THRUST_POCKET_H))\nbot = bot.cut(radial_pocket)\n\n# Shaft through-hole (full height)\nthrough = Part.makeCylinder(SHAFT_CLEAR / 2.0, BOT_H + 2)\nthrough.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(0, 0, -1))\nbot = bot.cut(through)\n\n# Small cutout in bottom for shaft end clearance (if shaft extends past thrust)\nshaft_clearance_bottom = Part.makeCylinder(SHAFT_D / 2.0 + 1.0, 4.0)\nshaft_clearance_bottom.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(0, 0, -1))\nbot = bot.cut(shaft_clearance_bottom)\n\n# 4\u00d7 M5 bolt holes with countersink on top (mount face UP \u2014 bolts thread down through plate)\n# Bottom block mounts UNDER the aluminum plate, bolts come from above\nfor i in range(4):\n    ang = math.radians(i * 90.0 + 45.0)\n    bx = (BOT_BOLT_PCD / 2.0) * math.cos(ang)\n    by = (BOT_BOLT_PCD / 2.0) * math.sin(ang)\n    bh = Part.makeCylinder(BOT_BOLT_D / 2.0, BOT_H + 2)\n    bh.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(bx, by, -1))\n    bot = bot.cut(bh)\n    # Countersink on TOP (facing up toward the plate)\n    cs = Part.makeCylinder(BOT_BOLT_CS_D / 2.0, 3.5)\n    cs.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(bx, by, BOT_H - 3.5))\n    bot = bot.cut(cs)\n\n# Position BOTTOM block for visualization\nbot.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(50, 0, 0))\n\nobj_bot = doc.addObject(\"Part::Feature\", \"BearingHousing_BOTTOM\")\nobj_bot.Shape = bot\nobj_bot.ViewObject.ShapeColor = (0.85, 0.45, 0.25)\nprint(f\"  BOTTOM: {BOT_W}x{BOT_D}x{BOT_H}mm\")\nprint(f\"  Thrust pocket: \u00d8{BOT_THRUST_POCKET_D}mm \u00d7 {BOT_THRUST_POCKET_H}mm (bottom)\")\nprint(f\"  Radial pocket: \u00d8{BOT_RADIAL_POCKET_D}mm \u00d7 {BOT_RADIAL_POCKET_H}mm (above thrust)\")\nprint(f\"  Shaft bore: \u00d8{SHAFT_CLEAR}mm\")\nprint(f\"  Volume: {bot.Volume:.0f}mm\u00b3\")\n\ndoc.recompute()\n\n# =============================================================================\n# EXPORT 1 \u2014 STL (individual + pair)\n# =============================================================================\nprint(f\"\\nExporting STL files...\")\n\n# Reset positions for proper export (each part at origin)\ntop_export = obj_top.Shape.copy()\ntop_export.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(50, 0, 0))  # cancel the -50 offset\n\nbot_export = obj_bot.Shape.copy()\nbot_export.translate(FreeCAD.Vector(-50, 0, 0))  # cancel the +50 offset\n\ntry:\n    # Individual STLs\n    tmp_top = doc.addObject(\"Part::Feature\", \"tmp_top\")\n    tmp_top.Shape = top_export\n    Part.export([tmp_top], PATH_STL_TOP)\n    doc.removeObject(\"tmp_top\")\n    print(f\"  \u2705 TOP STL:    {PATH_STL_TOP}\")\n\n    tmp_bot = doc.addObject(\"Part::Feature\", \"tmp_bot\")\n    tmp_bot.Shape = bot_export\n    Part.export([tmp_bot], PATH_STL_BOT)\n    doc.removeObject(\"tmp_bot\")\n    print(f\"  \u2705 BOTTOM STL: {PATH_STL_BOT}\")\n\n    # Pair STL (both together, as positioned in view)\n    Part.export([obj_top, obj_bot], PATH_STL_PAIR)\n    print(f\"  \u2705 PAIR STL:   {PATH_STL_PAIR}\")\n\nexcept Exception as e:\n    print(f\"  \u274c STL export error: {e}\")\n\n# If your printer produces 28.00mm pockets when you asked for 27.95mm, you adjust all future press fits accordingly.\n\nWhat comes next after this? Every future part will come packaged the same way.\n\n## Next up: NEMA 23 motor mount\n\nLet me walk through the engineering first so you can confirm the design direction before I write the macro.\n\n### What the motor mount does\n\nIt rigidly holds your NEMA 23 + 5:1 planetary gearbox to a 2020 aluminum extrusion upright, positioned so the 20T GT2 pulley on the gearbox output shaft aligns vertically with the 60T GT2 pulley on your main shaft drive hub. The mount must:\n\n1. Hold the motor square to the shaft (no tilt = no belt misalignment)\n2. Provide **belt tensioning adjustment** \u2014 the motor needs to slide horizontally relative to the main shaft to tension the belt\n3. Dissipate motor heat (stepper + gearbox can reach 50\u201360\u00b0C sustained)\n4. Bolt cleanly to 2020 extrusion using standard M5 T-nuts\n\n### NEMA 23 + gearbox dimensions (standard)\n\n| Feature | Value |\n|---|---|\n| NEMA 23 faceplate | 57mm \u00d7 57mm |\n| Bolt pattern | 47.14mm square (M5 bolts) |\n| Pilot boss diameter | \u00d838.1mm |\n| Gearbox output shaft | \u00d810mm (with 5:1 gearbox) |\n| Combined length (motor + gearbox) | ~150\u2013180mm depending on brand |\n\n### Design options \u2014 pick one\n\n**Option A \u2014 Simple fixed bracket**\n- Motor bolts to L-shaped mount\n- Belt tension set by shimming or moving the mount on the extrusion\n- Simplest, cheapest, but tensioning is awkward\n\n**Option B \u2014 Slotted mount (recommended)**\n- L-bracket with vertical slots where motor bolts pass through\n- Motor can slide up/down to tension the belt\n- Loosen bolts \u2192 slide motor \u2192 retighten\n- Industry-standard approach\n\n**Option C \u2014 Hinged mount with tension screw**\n- Motor mounted on a plate that pivots on one edge\n- Tension screw pushes motor away from main shaft\n- Most precise tension control, most complex to print/assemble\n\n### My recommendation: Option B (slotted)\n\nIt's the right balance of simplicity and function for a PETG prototype. You can upgrade to Option C later if belt tension proves hard to maintain.     "start_timestamp": "2026-04-22T04:12:30.394123Z",
    "stop_timestamp": "2026-04-22T04:12:43.380784Z",
    "flags": null,
    "type": "text",
    "text": " Let me review this carefully before you order.\n\n## Assessment: **This one will work for the top collar, but is NOT ideal for the bottom collar under the cassette.**\n\nHere's the engineering reasoning:\n\n### What the collar does in your machine\n\nYou have two collars on the 12mm shaft:\n\n1. **Top collar** \u2014 sits above the top bearing, prevents shaft from sliding downward through the bearings\n2. **Bottom collar (critical)** \u2014 sits below the drive hub / thrust bearing, locks the entire cassette + drive assembly axially against gravity and belt tension\n\nThe bottom collar carries real load. It holds up ~600g of cassette + resists vertical belt tension component (~5-10N).\n\n### Problem with set screw collars on precision shafts\n\nThe Harfington product is a **set screw collar**. The way it grips:\n- A single M4 or M5 set screw presses against the shaft\n- The screw tip creates a small dent/mark on the shaft surface\n- Grip relies on that single point of contact\n\n**Issues:**\n1. **Damages your ground shaft** \u2014 the set screw dents the 12mm precision ground surface. Once dented, the shaft is harder to re-position cleanly.\n2. **Can slip under load** \u2014 set screw collars are rated for light axial loads only. Industrial specs typically say \"not for precision positioning under load.\"\n3. **Vibration loosening** \u2014 at 60+ RPM with belt pulsing, set screws work loose over time. Requires Loctite 243 + regular re-torquing.\n\n### Better option: clamping (split) collars\n\nA **clamping collar** wraps around the shaft and squeezes shut with a clamp screw. No contact with the shaft surface in a single point \u2014 it grips over the entire inner circumference.\n\n**Advantages:**\n- No shaft damage (no dent, no mark)\n- Grips 3-5\u00d7 harder than set screw\n- Does not loosen under vibration\n- Repositionable infinitely without marking shaft\n- Industry standard for precision shaft assemblies\n\n### Recommended products\n\nLet me search for the right parts:",

The two basic types of shaft collars are clamping (or split) collars, which come in one- or two-piece designs, and setscrew collars. In both types, one or more screws hold the collars in place on the shaft. In setscrew collars, screws are tightened through the collar until they press directly against the shaft, and in clamping collars, screws are tightened to uniformly compress the collar around the shaft without impinging or marring it. Setscrew collars and one-piece clamping collars must be installed by sliding the collar over the end of the shaft, while two-piece clamping collars separate into two halves and can be installed between components on the shaft. Shaft collars are made from a wide range of materials including zinc-plated steel, aluminum, nylon, and neoprene.\n\n\n\nOne-piece clamping shaft collar for applications requiring",

There are many clones on Amazon with the exact same name. Saving $25 on a clone to save time/support issues isn't worth it. If one pin fails or USB chip acts flaky during motion control debugging, you'll spend many hours troubleshooting \u2014 more than the $25 savings.\n\nOne genuine board + one ELEGOO clone as backup (~$70 total) is the ideal setup. Clone goes in the drawer. If your genuine board ever fails, swap in the clone in 5 minutes without stopping the project.\n\n---\n\n## What's next after this\n\nTo finish the electronics order, you still need:\n\n| Item | Notes |\n|---|---|\n| Raspberry Pi 4 (4GB) | For touchscreen UI \u2014 already in BOM |\n| TB6600 stepper driver | Takes Arduino step/dir signals, drives NEMA 23 |\n| 24V/10A PSU | Powers the TB6600 + motor |\n| 5V buck converter | Powers Arduino from 24V rail |\n| Logic level shifter | Only if you end up using Pi GPIO for anything \u2014 not needed for Pi\u2194Arduino UART |\n\nReady to verify any of these, or shall we continue with the motor mount macro whenever you send me the gearbox link?",

    "text": " Yes \u2014 that's the correct driver and a good match for your build. Let me verify it against your exact setup.\n\n## Specifications match your system\n\n| Feature | EASON TB6600 | Your requirement | Match |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Max current | 4.0 A | NEMA 23 at ~2.8\u20133.0 A | \u2705 Headroom |\n| Input voltage | DC 9\u201342 V | 24V PSU | \u2705 Middle of range |\n| Microstepping | Up to 32\u00d7 (1/32) | 1/16 planned | \u2705 Supports both |\n| Compatible motors | NEMA 17, 23, 24 | NEMA 23 | \u2705 |\n| Signal inputs | Pulse/Direction/Enable | Arduino Mega step+dir | \u2705 Standard |\n| Signal voltage | 5V TTL optoisolated | Arduino 5V logic | \u2705 |\n\n## Why TB6600 is the right driver for CSM\n\n**Current rating headroom.** Your NEMA 23 with gearbox draws ~2.8\u20133.0A peak. The TB6600 at 4A gives you ~30% headroom, which keeps the driver cool and extends life. Running a driver at 80%+ of rated current for hours causes thermal shutdown and MOSFET aging.\n\n**Optoisolated inputs.** The step/dir/enable pins are isolated from Arduino through optocouplers. This is critical because stepper drivers switch high currents that generate electrical noise \u2014 without isolation, noise travels back into the Arduino and corrupts motion commands. You'd see random missed steps and direction errors.\n\n**1/16 microstepping = smooth rotation.** At 60 RPM with 48,000 steps/rev (200 \u00d7 1/16 \u00d7 15 gear ratio), each microstep is 0.0075\u00b0 of cylinder rotation. This gives you the butter-smooth knitting motion you need \u2014 no visible step pulsation in the fabric.\n\n## How to wire it (quick reference)\n\n```\n24V PSU \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500\u252c\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 VCC (driver power)\n                \u2514\u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 GND\n\nArduino Mega \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 PUL+ (step signal, pin 3)\nArduino GND \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 PUL-\n\nArduino Mega \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 DIR+ (direction signal, pin 4)\nArduino GND \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 DIR-\n\nArduino Mega \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 ENA+ (enable signal, pin 5) [optional]\nArduino GND \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 ENA-\n\nStepper motor \u2500\u2500\u2500\u2500 A+, A-, B+, B-\n```\n\n## Critical settings on the DIP switches\n\nThe TB6600 has 6 DIP switches on top to set current and microstepping. You need to configure them correctly before first power-on:\n\n**Current setting (switches 1-3):**\n- Set to **2.8A** for your NEMA 23 (the 2.8A current setting on most TB6600 units)\n- Never exceed the motor's rated current or it burns out\n- Check the sticker on the driver \u2014 switch positions vary slightly by manufacturer\n\n**Microstepping (switches 4-6):**\n- Set to **1/16** (16 microsteps per full step)\n- This matches our locked spec of 48,000 steps/revolution at the cylinder\n\n## One small but important thing to check in the listing\n\nVerify the product photo shows the **EASON TB6600** has:\n- Aluminum heatsink case (not bare PCB \u2014 bare PCB versions overheat)\n- Clearly labeled DIP switches on top\n- 3 signal input terminals (PUL+/-, DIR+/-, ENA+/-)\n- 2 motor output terminals (A+/-, B+/-)\n- 2 power input terminals (VCC, GND)\n\nIf it has all of these \u2192 correct product. Price should be $15\u201320.\n\n## Honest note on TB6600 vs alternatives\n\nTB6600 is the **budget-friendly workhorse**. For your prototype it's perfect. However, for future reference:\n\n| Driver | Price | Best for |\n|---|---|---|\n| TB6600 (your choice) | $15\u201320 | Prototype, proven, simple |\n| DM542T | $35\u201345 | Production machine \u2014 quieter, smoother, better current control |\n| Closed-loop servo driver | $150+ | Industrial production \u2014 can detect missed steps |\n\n**My recommendation:** Buy the TB6600 now. It will work perfectly for prototyping through Phase 6. If later you find you need smoother operation or want to detect missed steps during production runs, upgrade to DM542T (same wiring, drop-in replacement). The TB6600 becomes your backup.\n\n---\n\n## Quick verification before you click buy\n\n\u2705 Rated 4A \u2014 correct for your NEMA 23 (leaves 30% headroom)\n\u2705 DC 9\u201342V input \u2014 matches your 24V PSU\n\u2705 Up to 32\u00d7 microstepping \u2014 supports our 1/16 setting\n\u2705 Single axis \u2014 you have one drive motor\n\u2705 NEMA 17/23 compatible \u2014 matches your motor\n\n**Order it.** This is the right part.",

Let me explain clearly.\n\n## Problem 1: Current rating mismatch with your TB6600 driver (critical)\n\n| Component | Current rating |\n|---|---|\n| This motor | **5.0 A** |\n| TB6600 driver | **4.0 A max** |\n\n**The TB6600 cannot deliver 5A to this motor.** You will have to set the driver to 4A (its maximum), which means the motor only produces **80% of its rated torque** \u2014 roughly 2.4 Nm instead of the advertised 3 Nm.\n\nMore importantly, the TB6600 will run at 100% of its current rating continuously \u2014 which is where drivers fail. TB6600s overheat and thermal-shutdown when run at max current for sustained periods. You'd see random mid-knit stops.\n\n## Problem 2: Torque oversized for your application (subtle but matters)\n\nWith a 5:1 gearbox:\n- Motor torque: 3 Nm\n- Output torque: **15 Nm at the gearbox shaft**\n- With 15:1 total ratio to cylinder (20T\u219260T belt): **~45 Nm at cylinder**\n\nYour cylinder only needs about **3\u20136 Nm of driving torque** at the cylinder (calculated from needle butt friction + belt tension + cassette inertia at 60 RPM). You'd have **8\u201310\u00d7 more torque than needed**.\n\nWhy this is bad:\n- The system becomes \"infinitely stiff\" \u2014 if a needle jams, the motor will break needles/cylinder rather than stall\n- Stiction/backlash effects become more pronounced\n- Belt tension has to be perfect or pulley teeth skip under the excess torque\n- Larger vibration at speed from higher rotating mass\n\n## The right motor for your build\n\nYou want a NEMA 23 rated at **2.8\u20133.0 A, around 1.9 Nm holding torque**.     "type": "text",
    "text": "I have very important findings. First, an important reality check on the small pulley:\n\n## \u26a0\ufe0f Important discovery \u2014 20T GT2 with 14mm bore is VERY rare\n\n**Why:** A GT2 20T pulley has an outer diameter of only ~12mm. You cannot physically drill a 14mm bore through a 12mm pulley \u2014 the math doesn't work. GT2 pulleys were designed for 3D printer small motors with small shafts (5\u20138mm). 14mm bore requires a larger tooth count.\n\nMost 14mm bore pulleys use either **HTD 5M** (larger pitch) or require a larger tooth count like 40T+.\n\n## Solution: Change belt system from GT2 to HTD 5M (better for your application anyway)\n\n**HTD 5M** is designed for higher torque transmission and larger shafts. It's the correct system for NEMA 23 gearbox drives at 9.5 Nm output torque. GT2 is designed for lightweight 3D printer motion \u2014 not your use case.\n\nLet me search for the right system:",

If no magnetic field is present the SS49E will output a voltage around half of the ...\n\nThe SS49E is a linear hall-effect sensor. It can measure both north and south polarity of a magnetic field and the relative strength of the field. The output pin provides an analog output representing if a magnetic field is present, how strong a present field is, and if it is a north or south polar field. If no magnetic field is present the SS49E will output a voltage around half of the source voltage. If the south pole of a magnet is placed near the labeled side of the SS49E (the side with text etched on it), then the output voltage will linearly ramp up towards the source voltage. The amount of the output voltage increase is proportional to the strength of the magnetic field applied. \n\nIf the north pole of a magnet is placed near the labeled side of the SS49E then the output voltage will linearly ramp down toward the ground voltage relative to the strength of the magnetic field.\n\nYou can easily use the SS49E with a microcontroller (such as Arduino) or single board computer (SBC). Just provide power to the GND and VCC pins of the SS49E and connect its output pin to an analog input on your microcontroller or SBC, which you can then measure the analog voltage of to calculate the sensor's measured data. So why use a Hall-effect sensor? Hall-effect sensors are immune to most environmental disturbances that may affect optical or mechanical devices, such as vibration, moisture, dirt or oil films, ambient lighting, etc. Also, they are a simple way to measure the presence of a magnet and even electrical current running through a conductor.
